People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m here because… honestly, I’m exhausted. For about a year now I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that parts of my dad’s lab results are being changed, or held back, somewhere in the clinic system. I know how that sounds, but I work around labs, I know what numbers and charts are supposed to look like, and what I saw before does not match what ends up being shown later.

Every time I bring it up, doctors say his numbers are fine, or my family tells me I’m overreacting, or that I misunderstood something, but I didn’t. I know what I saw. The chart doesn’t line up, and it feels like someone is smoothing it over or hiding pieces of it. I’ve been trying to prove it, trying to track who had access and when, and it’s just… it’s taking over everything and wearing me down.

Yes. I mean… I don’t know the exact person’s name, and that’s part of what’s driving me crazy, but yes, I think someone in the clinic system is either changing parts of the results or holding things back before the final chart is shown.

It’s not just me being nervous about my dad. I saw numbers and pieces of the chart that did not match what was there later. And every time I try to point that out, people act like I’m confused or like I don’t understand labs, but I do this for work. I know what these things are supposed to look like. Doctors keep saying his numbers are fine, my family keeps telling me to let it go, but I know what I saw. The chart doesn’t line up, and it feels like it’s being smoothed over somehow. I’ve spent so much time trying to figure out who had access, when something changed, where the missing pieces went… and it’s just worn me down completely.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d call it a larger conspiracy exactly… I’m not saying there’s some huge group or something with a plan written down. But I do think someone in the clinic system is doing something, yes. Changing parts of the results, or holding them back, or only letting certain parts show up in the final chart.

Because this has been going on for about a year, and I’ve been trying to prove it the whole time. It’s not just one little number I misread. I work around labs, I know what results are supposed to look like, and what I saw before does not match what everyone keeps showing me later. And every time I bring it up, it’s like the same response: doctors say my dad’s numbers are fine, my family says I’m overreacting, people act like I’m confused. But I know what I saw.

So… bigger plan? I don’t know. But something is being covered up or smoothed over in the clinic system, and it’s worn me down trying to figure out who touched the chart, when it changed, and why nobody will take that seriously.

But something is being covered up or smoothed over in the clinic system, and it’s worn me down trying to figure out who touched the chart, when it changed, and why nobody will take that seriously.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think this is happening to you and your dad specifically, or could it be happening to other patients as well?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 33 / phlebotomist / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because for the past year I’ve been trying to prove that someone in the clinic system is changing or holding back parts of my dad’s lab results, and no matter how many doctors or family members tell me the numbers are fine, I know the chart doesn’t match what I saw and it’s wearing me down.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I keep going back and forth on that. At first I thought it was just my dad, because it’s his chart, his results, and I’m the one who saw that things didn’t match later. So that’s the part I can talk about for sure — or as sure as I can be — that something with his labs is being changed or held back somewhere in the clinic system.

But then I think… if someone can do it with his results, why would it only be him? I don’t have proof about other patients, and I’m not trying to accuse the whole place of everything, but it makes me wonder. Because nobody will acknowledge even the obvious mismatch in his chart. The doctors keep saying his numbers are fine, my family keeps telling me I’m overreacting, and it’s like everyone wants me to just stop asking. But I know what I saw before, and it doesn’t line up with what’s showing now.

So I guess my honest answer is: I know it’s happening with my dad’s lab results, and I’m worried it could be happening to others too. But my focus has been on proving what happened with him, because that’s what has taken over my life for the past year.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
If they actually agreed there was a mistake and were really investigating it… I think that would help, yes. I mean, that’s what I’ve been asking for this whole time — for somebody to stop brushing me off and say, “Okay, something doesn’t match here, let’s look into it.”

But I don’t think I’d instantly be calm, if that’s what you mean. Because this has been going on for a year. I’ve spent so much time trying to prove that parts of my dad’s lab results were changed or held back somewhere in the clinic system, and every time I brought it up, I was told the numbers were fine, or that I was misunderstanding, or my family would say I needed to let it go. So if suddenly everyone agreed, I’d still want to know what happened, who accessed the chart, what was changed, what was missing, and why nobody took it seriously before.

So yes, I’d probably still be concerned until I saw the investigation actually explain the mismatch. But I wouldn’t feel so alone in it. The worst part has been knowing the chart doesn’t match what I saw and having everyone act like that can’t be true. It’s just worn me down.

I know how that sounds, but I work around labs, I know what numbers and charts are supposed to look like, and what I saw before does not match what ends up being shown later.\n\nEvery time I bring it up, doctors say his numbers are fine, or my family tells me I’m overreacting, or that I misunderstood something, but I didn’t. I know what I saw. The chart doesn’t line up, and it feels like someone is smoothing it over or hiding pieces of it. I’ve been trying to prove it, trying to track who had access and when, and it’s just… it’s taking over everything and wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think someone is intentionally changing or hiding your dad's lab results?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I don’t know the exact person’s name, and that’s part of what’s driving me crazy, but yes, I think someone in the clinic system is either changing parts of the results or holding things back before the final chart is shown.\n\nIt’s not just me being nervous about my dad. I saw numbers and pieces of the chart that did not match what was there later. And every time I try to point that out, people act like I’m confused or like I don’t understand labs, but I do this for work. I know what these things are supposed to look like. Doctors keep saying his numbers are fine, my family keeps telling me to let it go, but I know what I saw. The chart doesn’t line up, and it feels like it’s being smoothed over somehow. I’ve spent so much time trying to figure out who had access, when something changed, where the missing pieces went… and it’s just worn me down completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this situation with your dad's lab results is part of a larger conspiracy or plan?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a larger conspiracy exactly… I’m not saying there’s some huge group or something with a plan written down. But I do think someone in the clinic system is doing something, yes. Changing parts of the results, or holding them back, or only letting certain parts show up in the final chart.\n\nBecause this has been going on for about a year, and I’ve been trying to prove it the whole time. It’s not just one little number I misread. I work around labs, I know what results are supposed to look like, and what I saw before does not match what everyone keeps showing me later. And every time I bring it up, it’s like the same response: doctors say my dad’s numbers are fine, my family says I’m overreacting, people act like I’m confused. But I know what I saw.\n\nSo… bigger plan? I don’t know. But something is being covered up or smoothed over in the clinic system, and it’s worn me down trying to figure out who touched the chart, when it changed, and why nobody will take that seriously.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this is happening to you and your dad specifically, or could it be happening to other patients as well?\n\nPatient: I keep going back and forth on that. At first I thought it was just my dad, because it’s his chart, his results, and I’m the one who saw that things didn’t match later. So that’s the part I can talk about for sure — or as sure as I can be — that something with his labs is being changed or held back somewhere in the clinic system.\n\nBut then I think… if someone can do it with his results, why would it only be him? I don’t have proof about other patients, and I’m not trying to accuse the whole place of everything, but it makes me wonder. Because nobody will acknowledge even the obvious mismatch in his chart. The doctors keep saying his numbers are fine, my family keeps telling me I’m overreacting, and it’s like everyone wants me to just stop asking. But I know what I saw before, and it doesn’t line up with what’s showing now.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is: I know it’s happening with my dad’s lab results, and I’m worried it could be happening to others too. But my focus has been on proving what happened with him, because that’s what has taken over my life for the past year.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about your dad's lab results if everyone, including doctors and your family, agreed that there was a mistake and it was being investigated?\n\nPatient: If they actually agreed there was a mistake and were really investigating it… I think that would help, yes. I mean, that’s what I’ve been asking for this whole time — for somebody to stop brushing me off and say, “Okay, something doesn’t match here, let’s look into it.”\n\nBut I don’t think I’d instantly be calm, if that’s what you mean. Because this has been going on for a year. I’ve spent so much time trying to prove that parts of my dad’s lab results were changed or held back somewhere in the clinic system, and every time I brought it up, I was told the numbers were fine, or that I was misunderstanding, or my family would say I needed to let it go. So if suddenly everyone agreed, I’d still want to know what happened, who accessed the chart, what was changed, what was missing, and why nobody took it seriously before.\n\nSo yes, I’d probably still be concerned until I saw the investigation actually explain the mismatch. But I wouldn’t feel so alone in it. The worst part has been knowing the chart doesn’t match what I saw and having everyone act like that can’t be true. It’s just worn me down.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I mean… most people already do think it’s unreasonable, don’t they? That’s been the whole problem. The doctors keep telling me his numbers are fine, my family keeps saying I’m overreacting, like I’ve gotten stuck on something and I should just drop it.

But from where I’m sitting, it doesn’t feel irrational. I know what I saw. I work around labs, I know charts don’t just feel “off” for no reason. The results I remember seeing don’t match what’s being shown now, and I’ve been trying for the past year to prove that someone in the clinic system changed something or held parts back. So if most people call that unreasonable, I guess they’re saying that because they didn’t see what I saw, or because they trust the system more than I do right now.

I can understand that to someone on the outside it might look like I’m fixated on it. I know I’ve spent a lot of time on it, and it’s worn me down. But I don’t think it’s irrational to keep asking questions when my dad’s chart doesn’t match what I saw and everyone just keeps insisting everything is fine.

I’ve spent so much time trying to figure out who had access, when something changed, where the missing pieces went… and it’s just worn me down completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this situation with your dad's lab results is part of a larger conspiracy or plan?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a larger conspiracy exactly… I’m not saying there’s some huge group or something with a plan written down. Changing parts of the results, or holding them back, or only letting certain parts show up in the final chart.\n\nBecause this has been going on for about a year, and I’ve been trying to prove it the whole time. It’s not just one little number I misread. I work around labs, I know what results are supposed to look like, and what I saw before does not match what everyone keeps showing me later. And every time I bring it up, it’s like the same response: doctors say my dad’s numbers are fine, my family says I’m overreacting, people act like I’m confused. But I know what I saw.\n\nSo… bigger plan? I don’t know. But something is being covered up or smoothed over in the clinic system, and it’s worn me down trying to figure out who touched the chart, when it changed, and why nobody will take that seriously.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this is happening to you and your dad specifically, or could it be happening to other patients as well?\n\nPatient: I keep going back and forth on that. At first I thought it was just my dad, because it’s his chart, his results, and I’m the one who saw that things didn’t match later. So that’s the part I can talk about for sure — or as sure as I can be — that something with his labs is being changed or held back somewhere in the clinic system.\n\nBut then I think… if someone can do it with his results, why would it only be him? I don’t have proof about other patients, and I’m not trying to accuse the whole place of everything, but it makes me wonder. Because nobody will acknowledge even the obvious mismatch in his chart. The doctors keep saying his numbers are fine, my family keeps telling me I’m overreacting, and it’s like everyone wants me to just stop asking. But I know what I saw before, and it doesn’t line up with what’s showing now.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is: I know it’s happening with my dad’s lab results, and I’m worried it could be happening to others too. But my focus has been on proving what happened with him, because that’s what has taken over my life for the past year.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about your dad's lab results if everyone, including doctors and your family, agreed that there was a mistake and it was being investigated?\n\nPatient: If they actually agreed there was a mistake and were really investigating it… I think that would help, yes. I mean, that’s what I’ve been asking for this whole time — for somebody to stop brushing me off and say, “Okay, something doesn’t match here, let’s look into it.”\n\nBut I don’t think I’d instantly be calm, if that’s what you mean. Because this has been going on for a year. I’ve spent so much time trying to prove that parts of my dad’s lab results were changed or held back somewhere in the clinic system, and every time I brought it up, I was told the numbers were fine, or that I was misunderstanding, or my family would say I needed to let it go. So if suddenly everyone agreed, I’d still want to know what happened, who accessed the chart, what was changed, what was missing, and why nobody took it seriously before.\n\nSo yes, I’d probably still be concerned until I saw the investigation actually explain the mismatch. But I wouldn’t feel so alone in it. The worst part has been knowing the chart doesn’t match what I saw and having everyone act like that can’t be true. It’s just worn me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your dad's lab results would be considered irrational or unreasonable by most people?\n\nPatient: I mean… most people already do think it’s unreasonable, don’t they? That’s been the whole problem. The doctors keep telling me his numbers are fine, my family keeps saying I’m overreacting, like I’ve gotten stuck on something and I should just drop it.\n\nBut from where I’m sitting, it doesn’t feel irrational. I know what I saw. I work around labs, I know charts don’t just feel “off” for no reason. The results I remember seeing don’t match what’s being shown now, and I’ve been trying for the past year to prove that someone in the clinic system changed something or held parts back. So if most people call that unreasonable, I guess they’re saying that because they didn’t see what I saw, or because they trust the system more than I do right now.\n\nI can understand that to someone on the outside it might look like I’m fixated on it. I know I’ve spent a lot of time on it, and it’s worn me down. But I don’t think it’s irrational to keep asking questions when my dad’s chart doesn’t match what I saw and everyone just keeps insisting everything is fine.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… it has. I don’t really like admitting that, because then it sounds like everyone is right that I’m “too focused” on it, but yes, it’s affected things.

With my family, I can tell they’re starting to work around me. Like with my dad’s appointments, there are plans I find out about later, or they’ll say, “Oh, we already talked to the clinic,” or “We didn’t want to get you upset.” And to me that feels awful, because I’m the one who has been paying attention to the chart and the labs. I’m not trying to make things harder for them, I’m trying to protect him. But I can feel them leaving me out of some of the appointment planning now, and that just makes me more worried because then I don’t know what’s being said or what’s being documented.

And at work… I mean, I’m a phlebotomist, so records and labs are not some abstract thing to me. I know how important accuracy is. But I think people there have started seeing me as someone who gets too intense about record problems. I used to feel like I might be considered for more responsibility, maybe lab supervision at some point, but now I get the sense that’s not happening. Like they think if there’s a chart issue or documentation issue, I’m going to make it confrontational instead of just handling it quietly. Nobody has said it exactly like that, but you can feel when you’re not being looked at the same way anymore.

So yes, it’s affected my relationships and my daily life. I still think the lab issue is real, but I can’t pretend it hasn’t cost me things.

I know what I saw. The chart doesn’t line up, and it feels like someone is smoothing it over or hiding pieces of it. I’ve been trying to prove it, trying to track who had access and when, and it’s just… it’s taking over everything and wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think someone is intentionally changing or hiding your dad's lab results?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I don’t know the exact person’s name, and that’s part of what’s driving me crazy, but yes, I think someone in the clinic system is either changing parts of the results or holding things back before the final chart is shown.\n\nIt’s not just me being nervous about my dad. I saw numbers and pieces of the chart that did not match what was there later. And every time I try to point that out, people act like I’m confused or like I don’t understand labs, but I do this for work. I know what these things are supposed to look like. Doctors keep saying his numbers are fine, my family keeps telling me to let it go, but I know what I saw. The chart doesn’t line up, and it feels like it’s being smoothed over somehow. I’ve spent so much time trying to figure out who had access, when something changed, where the missing pieces went… and it’s just worn me down completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this situation with your dad's lab results is part of a larger conspiracy or plan?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a larger conspiracy exactly… I’m not saying there’s some huge group or something with a plan written down. But I do think someone in the clinic system is doing something, yes. Changing parts of the results, or holding them back, or only letting certain parts show up in the final chart.\n\nBecause this has been going on for about a year, and I’ve been trying to prove it the whole time. It’s not just one little number I misread. I work around labs, I know what results are supposed to look like, and what I saw before does not match what everyone keeps showing me later. And every time I bring it up, it’s like the same response: doctors say my dad’s numbers are fine, my family says I’m overreacting, people act like I’m confused. But I know what I saw.\n\nSo… bigger plan? I don’t know. But something is being covered up or smoothed over in the clinic system, and it’s worn me down trying to figure out who touched the chart, when it changed, and why nobody will take that seriously.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this is happening to you and your dad specifically, or could it be happening to other patients as well?\n\nPatient: I keep going back and forth on that. At first I thought it was just my dad, because it’s his chart, his results, and I’m the one who saw that things didn’t match later. So that’s the part I can talk about for sure — or as sure as I can be — that something with his labs is being changed or held back somewhere in the clinic system.\n\nBut then I think… if someone can do it with his results, why would it only be him? I don’t have proof about other patients, and I’m not trying to accuse the whole place of everything, but it makes me wonder. Because nobody will acknowledge even the obvious mismatch in his chart. The doctors keep saying his numbers are fine, my family keeps telling me I’m overreacting, and it’s like everyone wants me to just stop asking. But I know what I saw before, and it doesn’t line up with what’s showing now.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is: I know it’s happening with my dad’s lab results, and I’m worried it could be happening to others too. But my focus has been on proving what happened with him, because that’s what has taken over my life for the past year.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about your dad's lab results if everyone, including doctors and your family, agreed that there was a mistake and it was being investigated?\n\nPatient: If they actually agreed there was a mistake and were really investigating it… I think that would help, yes. I mean, that’s what I’ve been asking for this whole time — for somebody to stop brushing me off and say, “Okay, something doesn’t match here, let’s look into it.”\n\nBut I don’t think I’d instantly be calm, if that’s what you mean. Because this has been going on for a year. I’ve spent so much time trying to prove that parts of my dad’s lab results were changed or held back somewhere in the clinic system, and every time I brought it up, I was told the numbers were fine, or that I was misunderstanding, or my family would say I needed to let it go. So if suddenly everyone agreed, I’d still want to know what happened, who accessed the chart, what was changed, what was missing, and why nobody took it seriously before.\n\nSo yes, I’d probably still be concerned until I saw the investigation actually explain the mismatch. But I wouldn’t feel so alone in it. The worst part has been knowing the chart doesn’t match what I saw and having everyone act like that can’t be true. It’s just worn me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your dad's lab results would be considered irrational or unreasonable by most people?\n\nPatient: I mean… most people already do think it’s unreasonable, don’t they? That’s been the whole problem. The doctors keep telling me his numbers are fine, my family keeps saying I’m overreacting, like I’ve gotten stuck on something and I should just drop it.\n\nBut from where I’m sitting, it doesn’t feel irrational. I know what I saw. I work around labs, I know charts don’t just feel “off” for no reason. The results I remember seeing don’t match what’s being shown now, and I’ve been trying for the past year to prove that someone in the clinic system changed something or held parts back. So if most people call that unreasonable, I guess they’re saying that because they didn’t see what I saw, or because they trust the system more than I do right now.\n\nI can understand that to someone on the outside it might look like I’m fixated on it. I know I’ve spent a lot of time on it, and it’s worn me down. But I don’t think it’s irrational to keep asking questions when my dad’s chart doesn’t match what I saw and everyone just keeps insisting everything is fine.\n\nDoctor: Has your preoccupation with your dad's lab results been affecting your daily life or relationships in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I don’t really like admitting that, because then it sounds like everyone is right that I’m “too focused” on it, but yes, it’s affected things.\n\nWith my family, I can tell they’re starting to work around me. Like with my dad’s appointments, there are plans I find out about later, or they’ll say, “Oh, we already talked to the clinic,” or “We didn’t want to get you upset.” And to me that feels awful, because I’m the one who has been paying attention to the chart and the labs. I’m not trying to make things harder for them, I’m trying to protect him. But I can feel them leaving me out of some of the appointment planning now, and that just makes me more worried because then I don’t know what’s being said or what’s being documented.\n\nAnd at work… I mean, I’m a phlebotomist, so records and labs are not some abstract thing to me. I know how important accuracy is. But I think people there have started seeing me as someone who gets too intense about record problems. I used to feel like I might be considered for more responsibility, maybe lab supervision at some point, but now I get the sense that’s not happening. Like they think if there’s a chart issue or documentation issue, I’m going to make it confrontational instead of just handling it quietly. Nobody has said it exactly like that, but you can feel when you’re not being looked at the same way anymore.\n\nSo yes, it’s affected my relationships and my daily life. I still think the lab issue is real, but I can’t pretend it hasn’t cost me things.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F22", "F32.0", "F41.0"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

I still think the lab issue is real, but I can’t pretend it hasn’t cost me things.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you change your mind about your dad's lab results being altered if new evidence came out that contradicted your current beliefs?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 33 / phlebotomist / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because for the past year I’ve been trying to prove that someone in the clinic system is changing or holding back parts of my dad’s lab results, and no matter how many doctors or family members tell me the numbers are fine, I know the chart doesn’t match what I saw and it’s wearing me down.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… it would depend on what you mean by “evidence.” If it was just another doctor saying, “No, the numbers are fine,” then no, probably not, because I’ve heard that over and over for the past year and it hasn’t explained why the chart doesn’t match what I saw.

If there was actual evidence — like a clear audit trail, something showing who accessed the results, when they were entered, whether anything was corrected or withheld, and it really accounted for the mismatch — then I would try to take that seriously. I’m not trying to believe this for no reason. I’m here because I’ve spent all this time trying to prove that someone in the clinic system changed or held back parts of my dad’s lab results, and it’s exhausting. It’s worn me down. I don’t want to be fighting everybody about it.

But I can’t just change my mind because people tell me I should. That’s the part that makes me sound stubborn, I know. It’s just… I know what I saw, and the chart now doesn’t line up with that. So if new evidence contradicted me, it would have to actually explain that, not just dismiss it. If it did explain it in a way that made sense, maybe I could reconsider. But I’d need to see it for myself.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F32.0, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… honestly, I’m exhausted. For about a year now I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that parts of my dad’s lab results are being changed, or held back, somewhere in the clinic system. I know how that sounds, but I work around labs, I know what numbers and charts are supposed to look like, and what I saw before does not match what ends up being shown later.\n\nEvery time I bring it up, doctors say his numbers are fine, or my family tells me I’m overreacting, or that I misunderstood something, but I didn’t. I know what I saw. The chart doesn’t line up, and it feels like someone is smoothing it over or hiding pieces of it. I’ve been trying to prove it, trying to track who had access and when, and it’s just… it’s taking over everything and wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think someone is intentionally changing or hiding your dad's lab results?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I don’t know the exact person’s name, and that’s part of what’s driving me crazy, but yes, I think someone in the clinic system is either changing parts of the results or holding things back before the final chart is shown.\n\nIt’s not just me being nervous about my dad. I saw numbers and pieces of the chart that did not match what was there later. And every time I try to point that out, people act like I’m confused or like I don’t understand labs, but I do this for work. I know what these things are supposed to look like. Doctors keep saying his numbers are fine, my family keeps telling me to let it go, but I know what I saw. The chart doesn’t line up, and it feels like it’s being smoothed over somehow. I’ve spent so much time trying to figure out who had access, when something changed, where the missing pieces went… and it’s just worn me down completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this situation with your dad's lab results is part of a larger conspiracy or plan?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a larger conspiracy exactly… I’m not saying there’s some huge group or something with a plan written down. But I do think someone in the clinic system is doing something, yes. Changing parts of the results, or holding them back, or only letting certain parts show up in the final chart.\n\nBecause this has been going on for about a year, and I’ve been trying to prove it the whole time. It’s not just one little number I misread. I work around labs, I know what results are supposed to look like, and what I saw before does not match what everyone keeps showing me later. And every time I bring it up, it’s like the same response: doctors say my dad’s numbers are fine, my family says I’m overreacting, people act like I’m confused. But I know what I saw.\n\nSo… bigger plan? I don’t know. But something is being covered up or smoothed over in the clinic system, and it’s worn me down trying to figure out who touched the chart, when it changed, and why nobody will take that seriously.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this is happening to you and your dad specifically, or could it be happening to other patients as well?\n\nPatient: I keep going back and forth on that. At first I thought it was just my dad, because it’s his chart, his results, and I’m the one who saw that things didn’t match later. So that’s the part I can talk about for sure — or as sure as I can be — that something with his labs is being changed or held back somewhere in the clinic system.\n\nBut then I think… if someone can do it with his results, why would it only be him? I don’t have proof about other patients, and I’m not trying to accuse the whole place of everything, but it makes me wonder. Because nobody will acknowledge even the obvious mismatch in his chart. The doctors keep saying his numbers are fine, my family keeps telling me I’m overreacting, and it’s like everyone wants me to just stop asking. But I know what I saw before, and it doesn’t line up with what’s showing now.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is: I know it’s happening with my dad’s lab results, and I’m worried it could be happening to others too. But my focus has been on proving what happened with him, because that’s what has taken over my life for the past year.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about your dad's lab results if everyone, including doctors and your family, agreed that there was a mistake and it was being investigated?\n\nPatient: If they actually agreed there was a mistake and were really investigating it… I think that would help, yes. I mean, that’s what I’ve been asking for this whole time — for somebody to stop brushing me off and say, “Okay, something doesn’t match here, let’s look into it.”\n\nBut I don’t think I’d instantly be calm, if that’s what you mean. Because this has been going on for a year. I’ve spent so much time trying to prove that parts of my dad’s lab results were changed or held back somewhere in the clinic system, and every time I brought it up, I was told the numbers were fine, or that I was misunderstanding, or my family would say I needed to let it go. So if suddenly everyone agreed, I’d still want to know what happened, who accessed the chart, what was changed, what was missing, and why nobody took it seriously before.\n\nSo yes, I’d probably still be concerned until I saw the investigation actually explain the mismatch. But I wouldn’t feel so alone in it. The worst part has been knowing the chart doesn’t match what I saw and having everyone act like that can’t be true. It’s just worn me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your dad's lab results would be considered irrational or unreasonable by most people?\n\nPatient: I mean… most people already do think it’s unreasonable, don’t they? That’s been the whole problem. The doctors keep telling me his numbers are fine, my family keeps saying I’m overreacting, like I’ve gotten stuck on something and I should just drop it.\n\nBut from where I’m sitting, it doesn’t feel irrational. I know what I saw. I work around labs, I know charts don’t just feel “off” for no reason. The results I remember seeing don’t match what’s being shown now, and I’ve been trying for the past year to prove that someone in the clinic system changed something or held parts back. So if most people call that unreasonable, I guess they’re saying that because they didn’t see what I saw, or because they trust the system more than I do right now.\n\nI can understand that to someone on the outside it might look like I’m fixated on it. I know I’ve spent a lot of time on it, and it’s worn me down. But I don’t think it’s irrational to keep asking questions when my dad’s chart doesn’t match what I saw and everyone just keeps insisting everything is fine.\n\nDoctor: Has your preoccupation with your dad's lab results been affecting your daily life or relationships in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I don’t really like admitting that, because then it sounds like everyone is right that I’m “too focused” on it, but yes, it’s affected things.\n\nWith my family, I can tell they’re starting to work around me. Like with my dad’s appointments, there are plans I find out about later, or they’ll say, “Oh, we already talked to the clinic,” or “We didn’t want to get you upset.” And to me that feels awful, because I’m the one who has been paying attention to the chart and the labs. I’m not trying to make things harder for them, I’m trying to protect him. But I can feel them leaving me out of some of the appointment planning now, and that just makes me more worried because then I don’t know what’s being said or what’s being documented.\n\nAnd at work… I mean, I’m a phlebotomist, so records and labs are not some abstract thing to me. I know how important accuracy is. But I think people there have started seeing me as someone who gets too intense about record problems. I used to feel like I might be considered for more responsibility, maybe lab supervision at some point, but now I get the sense that’s not happening. Like they think if there’s a chart issue or documentation issue, I’m going to make it confrontational instead of just handling it quietly. Nobody has said it exactly like that, but you can feel when you’re not being looked at the same way anymore.\n\nSo yes, it’s affected my relationships and my daily life. I still think the lab issue is real, but I can’t pretend it hasn’t cost me things.\n\nDoctor: Would you change your mind about your dad's lab results being altered if new evidence came out that contradicted your current beliefs?\n\nPatient: I mean… it would depend on what you mean by “evidence.” If it was just another doctor saying, “No, the numbers are fine,” then no, probably not, because I’ve heard that over and over for the past year and it hasn’t explained why the chart doesn’t match what I saw.\n\nIf there was actual evidence — like a clear audit trail, something showing who accessed the results, when they were entered, whether anything was corrected or withheld, and it really accounted for the mismatch — then I would try to take that seriously. I’m not trying to believe this for no reason. I’m here because I’ve spent all this time trying to prove that someone in the clinic system changed or held back parts of my dad’s lab results, and it’s exhausting. It’s worn me down. I don’t want to be fighting everybody about it.\n\nBut I can’t just change my mind because people tell me I should. That’s the part that makes me sound stubborn, I know. It’s just… I know what I saw, and the chart now doesn’t line up with that. So if new evidence contradicted me, it would have to actually explain that, not just dismiss it. If it did explain it in a way that made sense, maybe I could reconsider. But I’d need to see it for myself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Nobody has said it exactly like that, but you can feel when you’re not being looked at the same way anymore.\n\nSo yes, it’s affected my relationships and my daily life. I still think the lab issue is real, but I can’t pretend it hasn’t cost me things.\n\nDoctor: Would you change your mind about your dad's lab results being altered if new evidence came out that contradicted your current beliefs?\n\nPatient: I mean… it would depend on what you mean by “evidence.” If it was just another doctor saying, “No, the numbers are fine,” then no, probably not, because I’ve heard that over and over for the past year and it hasn’t explained why the chart doesn’t match what I saw.\n\nIf there was actual evidence — like a clear audit trail, something showing who accessed the results, when they were entered, whether anything was corrected or withheld, and it really accounted for the mismatch — then I would try to take that seriously. I’m not trying to believe this for no reason. I’m here because I’ve spent all this time trying to prove that someone in the clinic system changed or held back parts of my dad’s lab results, and it’s exhausting. It’s worn me down. I don’t want to be fighting everybody about it.\n\nBut I can’t just change my mind because people tell me I should. That’s the part that makes me sound stubborn, I know. It’s just… I know what I saw, and the chart now doesn’t line up with that. So if new evidence contradicted me, it would have to actually explain that, not just dismiss it. If it did explain it in a way that made sense, maybe I could reconsider. But I’d need to see it for myself.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your belief about your dad's lab results would change if you were shown the original, unaltered records?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
If you showed me what you’re calling the original, unaltered records… I mean, I would look at them, of course. I’m not refusing to look. But I don’t think just handing me a printout and saying, “See, these are original,” would automatically change my mind.

That’s the problem I keep running into. For the past year I’ve been trying to prove that someone in the clinic system changed something or held parts of my dad’s lab results back, and every time I’m told, “No, the numbers are fine,” it doesn’t actually answer the part that bothers me. The chart I remember seeing doesn’t match what’s being shown now. That’s not something I can just erase because someone presents another copy and says it’s unaltered.

I’d need to know where those records came from, who had access, whether there’s an audit trail, whether anything was corrected or hidden before I saw it. Because if the system is the thing I’m worried about, then records from that same system don’t automatically reassure me, if that makes sense.

I know how that sounds. I know it sounds like I’m setting the bar too high, and maybe that’s why people get frustrated with me. But I’m here because this has been wearing me down. I don’t want to keep fighting doctors and my family. I just… I know what I saw, and until something explains why it doesn’t match the chart now, I don’t think my belief would really change.

At first I thought it was just my dad, because it’s his chart, his results, and I’m the one who saw that things didn’t match later. I don’t have proof about other patients, and I’m not trying to accuse the whole place of everything, but it makes me wonder. Because nobody will acknowledge even the obvious mismatch in his chart. The doctors keep saying his numbers are fine, my family keeps telling me I’m overreacting, and it’s like everyone wants me to just stop asking. But I know what I saw before, and it doesn’t line up with what’s showing now.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is: I know it’s happening with my dad’s lab results, and I’m worried it could be happening to others too. But my focus has been on proving what happened with him, because that’s what has taken over my life for the past year.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about your dad's lab results if everyone, including doctors and your family, agreed that there was a mistake and it was being investigated?\n\nPatient: If they actually agreed there was a mistake and were really investigating it… I think that would help, yes. I mean, that’s what I’ve been asking for this whole time — for somebody to stop brushing me off and say, “Okay, something doesn’t match here, let’s look into it.”\n\nBut I don’t think I’d instantly be calm, if that’s what you mean. Because this has been going on for a year. I’ve spent so much time trying to prove that parts of my dad’s lab results were changed or held back somewhere in the clinic system, and every time I brought it up, I was told the numbers were fine, or that I was misunderstanding, or my family would say I needed to let it go. So if suddenly everyone agreed, I’d still want to know what happened, who accessed the chart, what was changed, what was missing, and why nobody took it seriously before.\n\nSo yes, I’d probably still be concerned until I saw the investigation actually explain the mismatch. But I wouldn’t feel so alone in it. The worst part has been knowing the chart doesn’t match what I saw and having everyone act like that can’t be true. I know what I saw. I work around labs, I know charts don’t just feel “off” for no reason. The results I remember seeing don’t match what’s being shown now, and I’ve been trying for the past year to prove that someone in the clinic system changed something or held parts back. So if most people call that unreasonable, I guess they’re saying that because they didn’t see what I saw, or because they trust the system more than I do right now.\n\nI can understand that to someone on the outside it might look like I’m fixated on it. I know I’ve spent a lot of time on it, and it’s worn me down. But I don’t think it’s irrational to keep asking questions when my dad’s chart doesn’t match what I saw and everyone just keeps insisting everything is fine.\n\nDoctor: Has your preoccupation with your dad's lab results been affecting your daily life or relationships in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I don’t really like admitting that, because then it sounds like everyone is right that I’m “too focused” on it, but yes, it’s affected things.\n\nWith my family, I can tell they’re starting to work around me. Like with my dad’s appointments, there are plans I find out about later, or they’ll say, “Oh, we already talked to the clinic,” or “We didn’t want to get you upset.” And to me that feels awful, because I’m the one who has been paying attention to the chart and the labs. I’m not trying to make things harder for them, I’m trying to protect him. But I can feel them leaving me out of some of the appointment planning now, and that just makes me more worried because then I don’t know what’s being said or what’s being documented.\n\nAnd at work… I mean, I’m a phlebotomist, so records and labs are not some abstract thing to me. I know how important accuracy is. But I think people there have started seeing me as someone who gets too intense about record problems. I used to feel like I might be considered for more responsibility, maybe lab supervision at some point, but now I get the sense that’s not happening. Like they think if there’s a chart issue or documentation issue, I’m going to make it confrontational instead of just handling it quietly. Nobody has said it exactly like that, but you can feel when you’re not being looked at the same way anymore.\n\nSo yes, it’s affected my relationships and my daily life. I still think the lab issue is real, but I can’t pretend it hasn’t cost me things.\n\nDoctor: Would you change your mind about your dad's lab results being altered if new evidence came out that contradicted your current beliefs?\n\nPatient: I mean… it would depend on what you mean by “evidence.” If it was just another doctor saying, “No, the numbers are fine,” then no, probably not, because I’ve heard that over and over for the past year and it hasn’t explained why the chart doesn’t match what I saw.\n\nIf there was actual evidence — like a clear audit trail, something showing who accessed the results, when they were entered, whether anything was corrected or withheld, and it really accounted for the mismatch — then I would try to take that seriously. I’m not trying to believe this for no reason. I’m here because I’ve spent all this time trying to prove that someone in the clinic system changed or held back parts of my dad’s lab results, and it’s exhausting. It’s worn me down. I don’t want to be fighting everybody about it.\n\nBut I can’t just change my mind because people tell me I should. That’s the part that makes me sound stubborn, I know. It’s just… I know what I saw, and the chart now doesn’t line up with that. So if new evidence contradicted me, it would have to actually explain that, not just dismiss it. If it did explain it in a way that made sense, maybe I could reconsider. But I’d need to see it for myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your dad's lab results would change if you were shown the original, unaltered records?\n\nPatient: If you showed me what you’re calling the original, unaltered records… I mean, I would look at them, of course. I’m not refusing to look. But I don’t think just handing me a printout and saying, “See, these are original,” would automatically change my mind.\n\nThat’s the problem I keep running into. For the past year I’ve been trying to prove that someone in the clinic system changed something or held parts of my dad’s lab results back, and every time I’m told, “No, the numbers are fine,” it doesn’t actually answer the part that bothers me. The chart I remember seeing doesn’t match what’s being shown now. That’s not something I can just erase because someone presents another copy and says it’s unaltered.\n\nI’d need to know where those records came from, who had access, whether there’s an audit trail, whether anything was corrected or hidden before I saw it. Because if the system is the thing I’m worried about, then records from that same system don’t automatically reassure me, if that makes sense.\n\nI know how that sounds. I know it sounds like I’m setting the bar too high, and maybe that’s why people get frustrated with me. But I’m here because this has been wearing me down. I don’t want to keep fighting doctors and my family. I just… I know what I saw, and until something explains why it doesn’t match the chart now, I don’t think my belief would really change.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… some of it would be normal, yes. Like, if it’s your parent and you think something is wrong with their medical information, of course you’re going to be concerned. I work around labs, so to me it doesn’t feel strange to pay attention to numbers, dates, what’s in the chart, what got entered, what didn’t. Families question things all the time when they’re worried.

But I also know most people probably wouldn’t still be on it the way I am after this long. That’s where I get stuck. Because for the past year I’ve been trying to prove that someone in the clinic system is changing or holding back parts of my dad’s lab results, and everyone keeps acting like I should just accept that the numbers are fine. Doctors say it, my family says it, and they look at me like I’m making it bigger than it is.

But the chart doesn’t match what I saw. That’s the part I can’t get past. So would other people think it’s normal? Maybe at first, maybe if they understood how serious chart accuracy is. But I think a lot of people would say I’ve gone too far with it, or that I’m wearing myself out over something they don’t see.

And I am worn down. I’ll admit that. It’s exhausting to keep feeling like you’re the only one noticing something important and everyone else just wants you to stop talking about it. So… I don’t know if others would call it normal, but to me it still feels like the concern makes sense because I know what I saw.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F32.0, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… honestly, I’m exhausted. For about a year now I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that parts of my dad’s lab results are being changed, or held back, somewhere in the clinic system. I know how that sounds, but I work around labs, I know what numbers and charts are supposed to look like, and what I saw before does not match what ends up being shown later.\n\nEvery time I bring it up, doctors say his numbers are fine, or my family tells me I’m overreacting, or that I misunderstood something, but I didn’t. I know what I saw. The chart doesn’t line up, and it feels like someone is smoothing it over or hiding pieces of it. I’ve been trying to prove it, trying to track who had access and when, and it’s just… it’s taking over everything and wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think someone is intentionally changing or hiding your dad's lab results?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I don’t know the exact person’s name, and that’s part of what’s driving me crazy, but yes, I think someone in the clinic system is either changing parts of the results or holding things back before the final chart is shown.\n\nIt’s not just me being nervous about my dad. I saw numbers and pieces of the chart that did not match what was there later. And every time I try to point that out, people act like I’m confused or like I don’t understand labs, but I do this for work. I know what these things are supposed to look like. Doctors keep saying his numbers are fine, my family keeps telling me to let it go, but I know what I saw. The chart doesn’t line up, and it feels like it’s being smoothed over somehow. I’ve spent so much time trying to figure out who had access, when something changed, where the missing pieces went… and it’s just worn me down completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this situation with your dad's lab results is part of a larger conspiracy or plan?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a larger conspiracy exactly… I’m not saying there’s some huge group or something with a plan written down. But I do think someone in the clinic system is doing something, yes. Changing parts of the results, or holding them back, or only letting certain parts show up in the final chart.\n\nBecause this has been going on for about a year, and I’ve been trying to prove it the whole time. It’s not just one little number I misread. I work around labs, I know what results are supposed to look like, and what I saw before does not match what everyone keeps showing me later. And every time I bring it up, it’s like the same response: doctors say my dad’s numbers are fine, my family says I’m overreacting, people act like I’m confused. But I know what I saw.\n\nSo… bigger plan? I don’t know. But something is being covered up or smoothed over in the clinic system, and it’s worn me down trying to figure out who touched the chart, when it changed, and why nobody will take that seriously.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this is happening to you and your dad specifically, or could it be happening to other patients as well?\n\nPatient: I keep going back and forth on that. At first I thought it was just my dad, because it’s his chart, his results, and I’m the one who saw that things didn’t match later. So that’s the part I can talk about for sure — or as sure as I can be — that something with his labs is being changed or held back somewhere in the clinic system.\n\nBut then I think… if someone can do it with his results, why would it only be him? I don’t have proof about other patients, and I’m not trying to accuse the whole place of everything, but it makes me wonder. Because nobody will acknowledge even the obvious mismatch in his chart. The doctors keep saying his numbers are fine, my family keeps telling me I’m overreacting, and it’s like everyone wants me to just stop asking. But I know what I saw before, and it doesn’t line up with what’s showing now.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is: I know it’s happening with my dad’s lab results, and I’m worried it could be happening to others too. But my focus has been on proving what happened with him, because that’s what has taken over my life for the past year.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about your dad's lab results if everyone, including doctors and your family, agreed that there was a mistake and it was being investigated?\n\nPatient: If they actually agreed there was a mistake and were really investigating it… I think that would help, yes. I mean, that’s what I’ve been asking for this whole time — for somebody to stop brushing me off and say, “Okay, something doesn’t match here, let’s look into it.”\n\nBut I don’t think I’d instantly be calm, if that’s what you mean. Because this has been going on for a year. I’ve spent so much time trying to prove that parts of my dad’s lab results were changed or held back somewhere in the clinic system, and every time I brought it up, I was told the numbers were fine, or that I was misunderstanding, or my family would say I needed to let it go. So if suddenly everyone agreed, I’d still want to know what happened, who accessed the chart, what was changed, what was missing, and why nobody took it seriously before.\n\nSo yes, I’d probably still be concerned until I saw the investigation actually explain the mismatch. But I wouldn’t feel so alone in it. The worst part has been knowing the chart doesn’t match what I saw and having everyone act like that can’t be true. It’s just worn me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your dad's lab results would be considered irrational or unreasonable by most people?\n\nPatient: I mean… most people already do think it’s unreasonable, don’t they? That’s been the whole problem. The doctors keep telling me his numbers are fine, my family keeps saying I’m overreacting, like I’ve gotten stuck on something and I should just drop it.\n\nBut from where I’m sitting, it doesn’t feel irrational. I know what I saw. I work around labs, I know charts don’t just feel “off” for no reason. The results I remember seeing don’t match what’s being shown now, and I’ve been trying for the past year to prove that someone in the clinic system changed something or held parts back. So if most people call that unreasonable, I guess they’re saying that because they didn’t see what I saw, or because they trust the system more than I do right now.\n\nI can understand that to someone on the outside it might look like I’m fixated on it. I know I’ve spent a lot of time on it, and it’s worn me down. But I don’t think it’s irrational to keep asking questions when my dad’s chart doesn’t match what I saw and everyone just keeps insisting everything is fine.\n\nDoctor: Has your preoccupation with your dad's lab results been affecting your daily life or relationships in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I don’t really like admitting that, because then it sounds like everyone is right that I’m “too focused” on it, but yes, it’s affected things.\n\nWith my family, I can tell they’re starting to work around me. Like with my dad’s appointments, there are plans I find out about later, or they’ll say, “Oh, we already talked to the clinic,” or “We didn’t want to get you upset.” And to me that feels awful, because I’m the one who has been paying attention to the chart and the labs. I’m not trying to make things harder for them, I’m trying to protect him. But I can feel them leaving me out of some of the appointment planning now, and that just makes me more worried because then I don’t know what’s being said or what’s being documented.\n\nAnd at work… I mean, I’m a phlebotomist, so records and labs are not some abstract thing to me. I know how important accuracy is. But I think people there have started seeing me as someone who gets too intense about record problems. I used to feel like I might be considered for more responsibility, maybe lab supervision at some point, but now I get the sense that’s not happening. Like they think if there’s a chart issue or documentation issue, I’m going to make it confrontational instead of just handling it quietly. Nobody has said it exactly like that, but you can feel when you’re not being looked at the same way anymore.\n\nSo yes, it’s affected my relationships and my daily life. I still think the lab issue is real, but I can’t pretend it hasn’t cost me things.\n\nDoctor: Would you change your mind about your dad's lab results being altered if new evidence came out that contradicted your current beliefs?\n\nPatient: I mean… it would depend on what you mean by “evidence.” If it was just another doctor saying, “No, the numbers are fine,” then no, probably not, because I’ve heard that over and over for the past year and it hasn’t explained why the chart doesn’t match what I saw.\n\nIf there was actual evidence — like a clear audit trail, something showing who accessed the results, when they were entered, whether anything was corrected or withheld, and it really accounted for the mismatch — then I would try to take that seriously. I’m not trying to believe this for no reason. I’m here because I’ve spent all this time trying to prove that someone in the clinic system changed or held back parts of my dad’s lab results, and it’s exhausting. It’s worn me down. I don’t want to be fighting everybody about it.\n\nBut I can’t just change my mind because people tell me I should. That’s the part that makes me sound stubborn, I know. It’s just… I know what I saw, and the chart now doesn’t line up with that. So if new evidence contradicted me, it would have to actually explain that, not just dismiss it. If it did explain it in a way that made sense, maybe I could reconsider. But I’d need to see it for myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your dad's lab results would change if you were shown the original, unaltered records?\n\nPatient: If you showed me what you’re calling the original, unaltered records… I mean, I would look at them, of course. I’m not refusing to look. But I don’t think just handing me a printout and saying, “See, these are original,” would automatically change my mind.\n\nThat’s the problem I keep running into. For the past year I’ve been trying to prove that someone in the clinic system changed something or held parts of my dad’s lab results back, and every time I’m told, “No, the numbers are fine,” it doesn’t actually answer the part that bothers me. The chart I remember seeing doesn’t match what’s being shown now. That’s not something I can just erase because someone presents another copy and says it’s unaltered.\n\nI’d need to know where those records came from, who had access, whether there’s an audit trail, whether anything was corrected or hidden before I saw it. Because if the system is the thing I’m worried about, then records from that same system don’t automatically reassure me, if that makes sense.\n\nI know how that sounds. I know it sounds like I’m setting the bar too high, and maybe that’s why people get frustrated with me. But I’m here because this has been wearing me down. I don’t want to keep fighting doctors and my family. I just… I know what I saw, and until something explains why it doesn’t match the chart now, I don’t think my belief would really change.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about the lab results would be considered normal by others who have experienced similar situations with their family members?\n\nPatient: I think… some of it would be normal, yes. Like, if it’s your parent and you think something is wrong with their medical information, of course you’re going to be concerned. I work around labs, so to me it doesn’t feel strange to pay attention to numbers, dates, what’s in the chart, what got entered, what didn’t. Families question things all the time when they’re worried.\n\nBut I also know most people probably wouldn’t still be on it the way I am after this long. That’s where I get stuck. Because for the past year I’ve been trying to prove that someone in the clinic system is changing or holding back parts of my dad’s lab results, and everyone keeps acting like I should just accept that the numbers are fine. Doctors say it, my family says it, and they look at me like I’m making it bigger than it is.\n\nBut the chart doesn’t match what I saw. That’s the part I can’t get past. So would other people think it’s normal? Maybe at first, maybe if they understood how serious chart accuracy is. But I think a lot of people would say I’ve gone too far with it, or that I’m wearing myself out over something they don’t see.\n\nAnd I am worn down. I’ll admit that. It’s exhausting to keep feeling like you’re the only one noticing something important and everyone else just wants you to stop talking about it. So… I don’t know if others would call it normal, but to me it still feels like the concern makes sense because I know what I saw.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

It’s just… I know what I saw, and the chart now doesn’t line up with that. So if new evidence contradicted me, it would have to actually explain that, not just dismiss it.
